47:- **Timing side-channel on `/identity`'s fail-closed error, partially mitigated, accepted residual (found by Tobias Lindqvist during LT-41 refinement, ruled 2026-09-13).** The identical-error requirement for "no token" / "bad token" / "vendor rejected it" (§1) covers response *body and status code*, but the three paths have inherently different latencies: no-token and bad-token fail locally (microseconds), vendor-rejected requires an actual round-trip to the third-party IDP (tens–hundreds of ms, and variable — the vendor's latency is not under our control). An attacker can distinguish "malformed/missing token" from "well-formed enough to reach the vendor" via timing alone, regardless of identical bodies.
49:  **This is a materially lower-severity leak than the classic timing side-channel this track has otherwise guarded against (e.g., password/account-existence timing, which this design closes by always running the same fixed-cost hash regardless of outcome).** What's disclosed here is only that a presented string has a shape that reached the vendor — not whether any specific identity or account exists, not PII, and not which part of a credential was wrong (§4's existing rule already normalizes vendor-rejection reasons). It's recon on token *shape*, not on any subject's identity.
51:  **Mitigation applied, not full closure: a response-time floor (~100ms, well under any `context.WithTimeout` budget in the call chain, so it doesn't interact with the timeout requirements elsewhere) applied uniformly across all three failure branches** — padding the fast local-fail paths up to roughly the floor narrows the trivially-observable microseconds-vs-hundreds-of-ms gap into something requiring statistical analysis across many requests rather than one request telling an attacker everything instantly. **Full closure is not pursued and is accepted as a residual**, because the vendor round-trip's latency *distribution* (not just its average) varies with real-world network and vendor conditions in ways a fixed floor cannot fully mask — unlike a local hash computation, which this design can and does make constant-time-equivalent regardless of outcome, a third-party network call's tail latency isn't ours to control. Closing this further would mean either delaying every request to match the vendor's worst observed latency (a real availability cost for a low-value information leak) or removing the network call from the timing entirely (impossible — we must actually ask the vendor). Accepted for this system on the grounds that the disclosed information is low-value token-shape recon, not identity or PII exposure, and the cost of full closure is disproportionate to that value.
58:- **Why this matters concretely:** without it, an unauthenticated caller could hit our `/auth` passthrough with arbitrary vendor credentials all day and use this connector as exactly the credential-testing oracle §4's rate-limit control is meant to prevent — but "rate-limited per calling-API-client" has nothing to bind "per calling-API-client" to if no caller identity is required to reach the endpoint at all.
65:This connector cannot enforce that boundary itself — the assignment's generic `/identity` contract (`{"phone": "...", "name": "..."}`, no scoping field) puts that decision entirely on the vendor's own API. **Stated assumption, not a resolved control:** this design assumes a real ABC/XYZ-shaped vendor binds `/identity` queries to the authenticated principal at the vendor side (i.e., the vendor's own API rejects a query for an identity other than the token-holder's). If that assumption doesn't hold for a given real vendor, this connector becomes a lookup oracle for anyone who can obtain one valid vendor token, and no control in §1–§5 touches that risk, because none of them govern token *authority* rather than token *custody*. This is the same class of question as the search API's object-level authorization (`api-auth-design.md`, S4) and belongs on `../01-product-industry-research-design/CLAUDE.md`'s list when evaluating which real vendor ABC/XYZ represents — this track cannot resolve it without knowing a concrete vendor's actual token semantics.
88:| 5 | Connector used as a credential-stuffing oracle against the vendor (rate-limit granularity unstated) | No per-identity/per-IP rate-limit granularity stated in the original design | M | M | **Control:** rate limit keyed per calling-API-client *and* per source IP, with a stricter threshold on vendor-auth *failure ratio* specifically (credential stuffing shows as a high failure ratio before high volume), paired with exponential backoff per identity attempted, not just per client — closes the granularity gap without adding a new requirement beyond what §4 already gestured at. | 03 (rate-limit logic) / 04 (shared rate-limit store or edge mechanism, if enforced at a gateway) | **L** | OWASP API4:2023; NIST SP 800-63B §5.2.2 |

Bash cat > /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-warrenthompson-Source-LoginID-02-ai-security-architecture/24287
input
{
  "command": "cat > /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-warrenthompson-Source-LoginID-02-ai-security-architecture/24287bc9-e284-45c4-9fca-81a6a4212461/scratchpad/pr40-review.md << 'EOF'\nIndependent review: Tomasz Wrede (02 Adversary) — PR #40 (LT-41, idp-connector `/auth`/`/identity`)\n\nRead the full diff. Three things checked, one real finding.\n\n**1. Fresh-Authorization-header-per-request — PASS, and it's structural, not test-shaped.**\n`httpvendorclient.go` builds a brand-new `*http.Request` via `http.NewRequestWithContext` inside `Authenticate`/`FetchIdentity` on every call, sets `Authorization` on that request object only, and never touches a client- or transport-level default header. `HTTPVendorClient` itself holds only `baseURL` (immutable string) and `httpClient` (a `*http.Client`, documented safe for concurrent use) as fields — there is no shared mutable state a concurrent-request race could corrupt, because nothing about the token is ever stored anywhere except as a local parameter passed straight into one request build. `Renata's test (`TestHTTPVendorClient_FetchIdentity_FreshAuthorizationHeaderPerRequest`) is sequential, correctly noted, but the reason it doesn't need to be concurrent to prove the property is that Go's connection-pool reuse (`http.Transport`) multiplexes TCP connections, never `*http.Request` objects or their headers — there's no code path here where two goroutines could contend over the same header. I'd still add a `-race`-run concurrent variant as cheap insurance against a future refactor reintroducing shared state (e.g., someone \"optimizing\" by hoisting a default header onto `defaultVendorHTTPClient` later), but this isn't a live gap today.\n\n**2. `GrantLimiter` reuse for vendor-call rate limiting — real gap, and it's a documented deviation from this project's own ruled control, not just my own preference. Flag as the one to fix.**\n`connector-security.md`'s own attack-tree row 5 (the credential-stuffing-oracle leaf S3/S8 chain already ruled on) specifies the control as: *\"rate limit keyed per calling-API-client and per source IP, with a stricter threshold on vendor-auth failure ratio specifically ... paired with exponential backoff per identity attempted, not just per client.\"* The shipped code (`connector_router.go`, `handlers.go`) keys the shared `GrantLimiter` instance on `(ac.Sub, sourceIP)` only — `ac.Sub` is the *calling internal API client's* subject (e.g., api-service's own service credential), not the end-user identity/username being tried against the vendor. The \"per identity attempted\" half of the ruled control is simply absent from this PR, with no comment naming the gap or an accepted-risk statement for it.\n\nWhy this matters concretely, not just as a documentation mismatch: this connector is called by a small number of trusted internal services sharing a small number of client credentials and source IPs — the effective `(sub, IP)` key space here is tiny, likely a single bucket for the whole connector's traffic in this project's own shape. That produces two failure modes at once, not one:\n- **Under-protective against a real attacker:** if the attacker's foothold is a compromised or malicious *internal caller* (the actual trust boundary this control is meant to defend), keying only by that caller's own sub does nothing to slow down testing many different stolen end-user credentials through it — they all share the same `(sub, IP)` bucket, but `RecordSuccess` on any one legitimate end-user login (which will happen regularly in normal traffic) resets the whole bucket's backoff, effectively laundering the attacker's failures.\n- **Over-protective against ordinary use:** the same coarse key means a handful of ordinary end-user password typos, all going through the one shared internal caller, can trip the shared backoff and rate-limit *every other unrelated end-user's* identity lookup behind that same caller — collateral denial-of-service from normal typo volume, not attacker behavior.\n\nBoth directions trace to the same root cause: the design explicitly named a second key dimension (identity attempted) as necessary to close this leaf, and the implementation dropped it silently. This should either be added (e.g., a secondary backoff bucket keyed on a salted/hashed form of the submitted username or phone — never the raw value, consistent with connector-security.md §3's never-log rule) or explicitly re-ruled as an accepted narrower scope with reasoning, the same way the timing floor below was.\n\n**3. ~100ms timing floor — PASS, framing matches the accepted-residual language exactly, applied uniformly, not over-claimed.**\n`identityFailureFloor`'s doc comment mirrors `connector-security.md` §4 near verbatim (\"narrows, but does not fully close ... full closure is an accepted non-goal\"), `waitForFloor` is called at both failure call sites that are part of the timing-oracle concern (no-token, and the merged bad-token/vendor-rejected path — the implementation actually collapses those into one code path rather than three, which reduces distinguishable branches versus the doc's three-way framing, a conservative deviation, not a risky one), and is correctly *not* applied to the 429 rate-limit branch (openly disclosed, not covert) or the malformed-JSON-body branch (not part of the named concern) or the success path. `TestNewIdentityHandler_AllThreeFailureBranches...` (name truncated in diff) checks all three named branches land within ~50ms of the floor, and a separate test confirms success is never floored. No gap, no over-claim.\n\nNet: 1 and 3 clean. 2 is the one that needs another pass — it's not a hypothetical concern, it's the project's own ruled control missing half its stated shape.\nEOF\ngh pr comment 40 --repo wtvamp/loginid-takehome --body-file /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-warrenthompson-Source-LoginID-02-ai-security-architecture/24287bc9-e284-45c4-9fca-81a6a4212461/scratchpad/pr40-review.md"

374	+package connector
375	+
376	+import (
377	+	"context"
378	+	"crypto/hmac"
379	+	"crypto/rand"
380	+	"crypto/sha256"
381	+	"encoding/hex"
382	+	"sync"
383	+	"time"
384	+
385	+	"loginid-takehome/internal/api"
386	+)
387	+
388	+// identityLimiterBaseBackoff/MaxBackoff/AlertThreshold are this
389	+// component's own engineering defaults, same reasoning as
390	+// connectorVendorLimiterBaseBackoff/etc. in internal/app/connector_router.go
391	+// — flagged for 02's review rather than treated as ruled numbers.
392	+const (
393	+	identityLimiterBaseBackoff    = 1 * time.Second
394	+	identityLimiterMaxBackoff     = 5 * time.Minute
395	+	identityLimiterAlertThreshold = 10
396	+)
397	+
398	+// maxTrackedIdentities bounds this limiter's own tracked-key set
399	+// independently of api.GrantLimiter's time-based sweep — the same
400	+// "bounded distinct-key cardinality" pattern LT-40's JWKSCache already
401	+// uses for unknown kids (jwksMaxTrackedUnknownKids), extended here to a
402	+// much larger value: unlike a kid (a small, bounded set of currently
403	+// valid signing keys), a legitimate identity space can genuinely be
404	+// large (many real vendor end-users), so capping at JWKS's own
405	+// small number would fail open on ordinary traffic almost immediately.
406	+// This is this component's own engineering default (flagged for 02's
407	+// review), not a ruled number.
408	+const maxTrackedIdentities = 10000
409	+
410	+// identityTrackedKeyTTL/identitySweepInterval bound how long a tracked
411	+// identity key survives with no further activity — same sweep-based
412	+// eviction shape as InProcessGrantLimiter/InProcessTouchCounter/
413	+// JWKSCache's own unknown-kid map, so a burst of one-off garbage
414	+// identities doesn't permanently consume tracked-key slots.
415	+const (
416	+	identityTrackedKeyTTL = time.Hour
417	+	identitySweepInterval = time.Minute
418	+)
419	+
420	+// identityHMACKey is generated once per process — this limiter's keys
421	+// never need to survive a restart (the state itself is in-process and
422	+// ephemeral), so a random key generated at process start is sufficient
423	+// and avoids embedding any fixed secret in source.
424	+var identityHMACKey = mustRandomBytes(32)
425	+
426	+func mustRandomBytes(n int) []byte {
427	+	b := make([]byte, n)
428	+	if _, err := rand.Read(b); err != nil {
429	+		panic("connector: generating identity-limiter HMAC key: " + err.Error())
430	+	}
431	+	return b
432	+}
433	+
434	+// hashIdentity derives this limiter's map key from (callerSub,
435	+// attemptedIdentity) — HMAC-SHA256, never the raw attempted identity
436	+// (a vendor username, or a phone/name pair) held as a map key or ever
437	+// logged (Tomasz Wrede's review, PR #40: "a salted/hashed submitted
438	+// identity, never the raw username/phone/whatever's being tried against
439	+// the vendor"). Composite over BOTH callerSub and attemptedIdentity —
440	+// per Marcus Ilori's ruling — so two different internal callers
441	+// submitting the same identity string never share one bucket, even
442	+// though this project's actual topology has only one internal caller
443	+// today.
444	+func hashIdentity(callerSub, attemptedIdentity string) string {
445	+	mac := hmac.New(sha256.New, identityHMACKey)
446	+	mac.Write([]byte(callerSub))
447	+	mac.Write([]byte{0}) // separator: prevents ("ab","c") colliding with ("a","bc")
448	+	mac.Write([]byte(attemptedIdentity))
449	+	return hex.EncodeToString(mac.Sum(nil))
450	+}
451	+
452	+// identityRateLimiter is attack-tree leaf 5's missing second dimension
453	+// (Tomasz Wrede's cold review of PR #40): connector-security.md's own
454	+// ruled control specifies backoff "per identity attempted, not just per
455	+// client" — the caller-level GrantLimiter instance
456	+// (internal/app/connector_router.go) alone means a single success
457	+// anywhere resets that caller's ENTIRE consecutive-failure count,
458	+// letting a compromised caller cycle through many stolen identities
459	+// indefinitely as long as it occasionally succeeds against one of them.
460	+// This type tracks backoff per (caller, attempted identity) instead, so
461	+// retrying the SAME identity trips independently of whether some OTHER
462	+// identity under the same caller just succeeded — and, symmetrically,
463	+// one legitimate user's typo never affects a different legitimate
464	+// user's own attempt under the same caller (both are named,
465	+// enforcement-tested properties, not just a design intent).
466	+//
467	+// Wraps api.GrantLimiter (not a new backoff mechanism — the hashed
468	+// identity is used as BOTH of GrantLimiter's own two key slots, since
469	+// neither "client_id" nor "source IP" is the right name for what this
470	+// tracks; using the same value for both is harmless, just double
471	+// bookkeeping for one entity) with an explicit, bounded tracked-key set
472	+// so an attacker submitting unbounded distinct garbage identities can't
473	+// grow memory without limit between GrantLimiter's own periodic sweeps.
474	+type identityRateLimiter struct {
475	+	underlying *api.InProcessGrantLimiter
476	+
477	+	mu        sync.Mutex
478	+	trackedAt map[string]time.Time
479	+	lastSweep time.Time
480	+}
481	+
482	+func NewIdentityRateLimiter() *identityRateLimiter {
483	+	return &identityRateLimiter{
484	+		underlying: api.NewInProcessGrantLimiter(
485	+			identityLimiterBaseBackoff, identityLimiterMaxBackoff, identityLimiterAlertThreshold, nil,
486	+		),
487	+		trackedAt: make(map[string]time.Time),
488	+	}
489	+}
490	+
491	+// allow reports whether an attempt against attemptedIdentity by
492	+// callerSub may proceed. Once maxTrackedIdentities distinct keys are
493	+// already tracked, a genuinely NEW identity is allowed through this
494	+// limiter (fails open on THIS dimension only) rather than blocking
495	+// every new identity outright — the caller-level GrantLimiter still
496	+// provides a base rate limit regardless, so this degrades to
497	+// caller-level-only protection under a tracked-key flood rather than
498	+// denying service to legitimate new identities (same reasoning as
499	+// JWKSCache's own bounded unknown-kid cap).
500	+func (l *identityRateLimiter) allow(ctx context.Context, callerSub, attemptedIdentity string) (bool, error) {
501	+	key := hashIdentity(callerSub, attemptedIdentity)
502	+
503	+	l.mu.Lock()
504	+	now := time.Now()
505	+	l.sweepLocked(now)
506	+	if _, tracked := l.trackedAt[key]; !tracked {
507	+		if len(l.trackedAt) >= maxTrackedIdentities {
508	+			l.mu.Unlock()
509	+			return true, nil
510	+		}
511	+		l.trackedAt[key] = now
512	+	} else {
513	+		l.trackedAt[key] = now
514	+	}
515	+	l.mu.Unlock()
516	+
517	+	return l.underlying.Allow(ctx, key, key)
518	+}
519	+
520	+func (l *identityRateLimiter) recordFailure(ctx context.Context, callerSub, attemptedIdentity string) {
521	+	key := hashIdentity(callerSub, attemptedIdentity)
522	+	l.underlying.RecordFailure(ctx, key, key)
523	+}
524	+
525	+func (l *identityRateLimiter) recordSuccess(ctx context.Context, callerSub, attemptedIdentity string) {
526	+	key := hashIdentity(callerSub, attemptedIdentity)
527	+	l.underlying.RecordSuccess(ctx, key, key)
528	+}
529	+
530	+func (l *identityRateLimiter) sweepLocked(now time.Time) {
531	+	if now.Sub(l.lastSweep) < identitySweepInterval {
532	+		return
533	+	}
534	+	l.lastSweep = now
535	+	for k, seenAt := range l.trackedAt {
536	+		if now.Sub(seenAt) >= identityTrackedKeyTTL {
537	+			delete(l.trackedAt, k)
538	+		}
539	+	}
540	+}

547	+package connector
548	+
549	+import (
550	+	"context"
551	+	"testing"
552	+)
553	+
554	+// TestIdentityRateLimiter_StolenIdentitiesSurviveAnUnrelatedSuccess is
555	+// Tomasz Wrede's named attack scenario (cold review of PR #40): the
556	+// caller-level GrantLimiter alone resets its ENTIRE consecutive-failure
557	+// count on any success, so a compromised caller cycling through many
558	+// stolen identities can keep a fresh allowance indefinitely as long as
559	+// it occasionally succeeds against ONE of them (e.g. one seed valid
560	+// account). Per-(caller, identity) keying must not have that property:
561	+// a specific identity's own backoff must trip on its own repeated
562	+// failures and must NOT be reset by a completely different identity
563	+// succeeding under the same caller.
564	+func TestIdentityRateLimiter_StolenIdentitiesSurviveAnUnrelatedSuccess(t *testing.T) {
565	+	l := NewIdentityRateLimiter()
566	+	ctx := context.Background()
567	+	const caller = "api-service"
568	+
569	+	// Fail against "victim-1" enough times to trip its own backoff.
570	+	blockedAfter := -1
571	+	for i := 0; i < 10; i++ {
572	+		allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, caller, "victim-1")
573	+		if err != nil {
574	+			t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
575	+		}
576	+		if !allowed {
577	+			blockedAfter = i
578	+			break
579	+		}
580	+		l.recordFailure(ctx, caller, "victim-1")
581	+	}
582	+	if blockedAfter == -1 {
583	+		t.Fatalf("victim-1 was never blocked across 10 consecutive failures")
584	+	}
585	+
586	+	// The attacker now succeeds against a COMPLETELY DIFFERENT identity
587	+	// under the same caller (the "one valid seed account" case).
588	+	allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, caller, "victim-2")
589	+	if err != nil {
590	+		t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
591	+	}
592	+	if !allowed {
593	+		t.Fatalf("victim-2 (never attempted before) was blocked — test setup bug")
594	+	}
595	+	l.recordSuccess(ctx, caller, "victim-2")
596	+
597	+	// victim-1 must STILL be blocked — victim-2's success must not have
598	+	// reset it. This is exactly the property a single shared per-caller
599	+	// bucket does NOT have (a success there resets everything).
600	+	allowed, err = l.allow(ctx, caller, "victim-1")
601	+	if err != nil {
602	+		t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
603	+	}
604	+	if allowed {
605	+		t.Errorf("victim-1 is allowed again after an unrelated identity (victim-2) succeeded — a per-identity bucket must not be resettable by a different identity's success")
606	+	}
607	+}
608	+
609	+// TestIdentityRateLimiter_TypoIsolation is Tomasz Wrede's other named
610	+// scenario: two different LEGITIMATE users behind the same caller, one
611	+// of whom mistypes their credential once, must not affect the other —
612	+// "strangers' typos" must not over-limit an unrelated identity.
613	+func TestIdentityRateLimiter_TypoIsolation(t *testing.T) {
614	+	l := NewIdentityRateLimiter()
615	+	ctx := context.Background()
616	+	const caller = "api-service"
617	+
618	+	// user-A mistypes once — this correctly starts user-A's OWN backoff
619	+	// (same progressive-backoff-from-the-first-failure design as
620	+	// api.GrantLimiter elsewhere in this codebase; not itself the
621	+	// property under test here) — then eventually succeeds.
622	+	if allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, caller, "user-A"); err != nil || !allowed {
623	+		t.Fatalf("user-A first attempt: allowed=%v err=%v", allowed, err)
624	+	}
625	+	l.recordFailure(ctx, caller, "user-A")
626	+	l.recordSuccess(ctx, caller, "user-A")
627	+
628	+	// user-B, a completely different identity under the same caller,
629	+	// must never have been affected by user-A's typo — first attempt
630	+	// must be allowed.
631	+	allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, caller, "user-B")
632	+	if err != nil {
633	+		t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
634	+	}
635	+	if !allowed {
636	+		t.Errorf("user-B was blocked by user-A's unrelated typo under the same caller — per-identity isolation must prevent this")
637	+	}
638	+}
639	+
640	+func TestIdentityRateLimiter_DifferentCallersSameIdentityDoNotShareState(t *testing.T) {
641	+	l := NewIdentityRateLimiter()
642	+	ctx := context.Background()
643	+
644	+	// caller-1 fails repeatedly against "shared-name" (both callers
645	+	// happen to submit the same attempted-identity string).
646	+	for i := 0; i < 10; i++ {
647	+		allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, "caller-1", "shared-name")
648	+		if err != nil {
649	+			t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
650	+		}
651	+		if !allowed {
652	+			break
653	+		}
654	+		l.recordFailure(ctx, "caller-1", "shared-name")
655	+	}
656	+
657	+	// caller-2 attempting the SAME identity string must not be affected
658	+	// — the key is composite over (caller, identity), per Marcus Ilori's
659	+	// ruling, not identity alone.
660	+	allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, "caller-2", "shared-name")
661	+	if err != nil {
662	+		t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
663	+	}
664	+	if !allowed {
665	+		t.Errorf("caller-2 was blocked by caller-1's failures against the same identity string — the key must be composite over (caller, identity)")
666	+	}
667	+}
668	+
669	+func TestIdentityRateLimiter_BoundedCardinality_DegradesOpenNotClosed(t *testing.T) {
670	+	l := NewIdentityRateLimiter()
671	+	ctx := context.Background()
672	+
673	+	// Flood past maxTrackedIdentities with distinct identities.
674	+	for i := 0; i < maxTrackedIdentities+10; i++ {
675	+		if _, err := l.allow(ctx, "flooding-caller", string(rune(i))+"-flood"); err != nil {
676	+			t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
677	+		}
678	+	}
679	+
680	+	// A brand-new identity past the cap must still be ALLOWED (fails
681	+	// open on this dimension only, per this type's own doc comment) —
682	+	// never silently denying service to a legitimate new identity just
683	+	// because the tracked-key set is full.
684	+	allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, "flooding-caller", "one-more-new-identity")
685	+	if err != nil {
686	+		t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
687	+	}
688	+	if !allowed {
689	+		t.Errorf("a new identity past maxTrackedIdentities was denied — this dimension must fail OPEN (allow), not closed, once the tracked-key cap is reached")
690	+	}
691	+}

{
  "command": "cat > /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-warrenthompson-Source-LoginID-02-ai-security-architecture/24287bc9-e284-45c4-9fca-81a6a4212461/scratchpad/pr40-recheck.md << 'EOF'\nIndependent review: Tomasz Wrede (02 Adversary) — PR #40 re-check at `5d67789`\n\n**My original finding 2 is closed. Both named scenarios are real, tested, and pass.**\n`identityRateLimiter` keys on `HMAC-SHA256(callerSub, attemptedIdentity)` — never the raw vendor username or phone/name — and is wired alongside, not instead of, the caller-level `GrantLimiter`. `TestIdentityRateLimiter_StolenIdentitiesSurviveAnUnrelatedSuccess` reproduces exactly the attack I named (compromised caller cycling stolen identities, occasionally succeeding against one seed account) and confirms the OTHER identity's backoff survives that unrelated success. `TestIdentityRateLimiter_TypoIsolation` confirms the opposite direction (one legitimate user's typo doesn't rate-limit a different legitimate user under the same caller). `TestIdentityRateLimiter_DifferentCallersSameIdentityDoNotShareState` is a good bonus check I didn't ask for but matches the design (\"composite over caller AND identity, per Marcus's ruling\") rather than identity alone, which would have been a different, subtler cross-tenant leak. Wiring in `handlers.go` gates before the vendor call in both handlers, correctly using `body.Username` for `/auth` and `phone|name` for `/identity`. This closes the gap connector-security.md's own ruled control specified and this PR's original version was missing.\n\n**The 10,000-key cap — you asked me to check this on my own initiative, and there is a real finding, but it's the opposite failure direction from the JWKS cap, and worth naming precisely.**\n\nUnlike JWKS's cap (which fails toward *rejection* when full — a bounded delay, never a bypass), this cap fails toward *allowance*: once 10,000 distinct `(caller, identity)` keys are tracked, a **brand-new** identity is let through with **no per-identity backoff applied at all** (`identityRateLimiter.allow`, the `len(l.trackedAt) >= maxTrackedIdentities` branch returns `true, nil` unconditionally) — confirmed and even explicitly tested as the intended behavior (`TestIdentityRateLimiter_BoundedCardinality_DegradesOpenNotClosed`). The design comment frames this as \"degrades to caller-level-only protection,\" which is true — but that caller-level-only state is *exactly* the weakness this whole fix exists to close (the original finding 2: one success anywhere resets the caller's whole bucket, so a compromised caller cycling many stolen identities defeats it as long as it occasionally succeeds against one).\n\nConcretely: an attacker doesn't need to attack real identities to trigger this. They can cheaply pre-fill the table — 10,000 distinct garbage `(caller, identity)` pairs, each touched at least once per `identityTrackedKeyTTL` (1 hour) to survive the sweep, which is roughly 3 requests/second sustained, trivial for even a small botnet, and doesn't require any valid vendor credential since a rejected `/auth` attempt still calls `identityGateRecordFailure` before the vendor rejects it — then proceed against real stolen identities, which now bypass identity-level backoff entirely and fall back to the exact caller-level bucket behavior finding 2 was written to fix. This isn't a hypothetical: the table is deliberately not evicting existing entries under pressure (good — that protects already-tracked legitimate identities from premature eviction, avoiding the JWKS-style mistake in that direction), but the flip side of that same design choice is that *new* identities past the cap get zero identity-level protection, which is precisely the dimension an attacker cares about exploiting.\n\nTo be fair to the design: refusing to evict existing tracked state to make room was the right call (protects already-established legitimate backoff state from a flood, unlike a naive LRU cap would), and *some* number has to degrade somehow once memory is bounded — there's no free lunch here. But \"degrades to caller-level-only\" needs to be read as \"degrades to the exact weakness finding 2 fixed,\" not as a soft, acceptable floor, and the 10,000 figure is called out in the code itself as \"this component's own engineering default... not a ruled number\" — so this is squarely a number Marcus should rule on with that framing in mind, the same way the JWKS staleness multiple and timing floor got explicit rulings. I'd want either a materially higher cap (raising the attacker's cost, not eliminating the shape) or a fallback response for the past-cap case that's stricter than \"identity-unlimited, caller-limit-only\" — e.g., a global cheap Bloom-filter-style \"have I seen many distinct new identities from this caller recently\" secondary signal — named as a follow-up rather than assumed closed.\n\nNet: identity-dimension gap from my first review — closed, both named scenarios pass. New finding: the cap's fail-open direction reintroduces a cheaper, attacker-triggerable path back to that same original weakness, and it's an unrolled engineering default rather than a ruled number.\nEOF\ngh pr comment 40 --repo wtvamp/loginid-takehome --body-file /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-warrenthompson-Source-LoginID-02-ai-security-architecture/24287bc9-e284-45c4-9fca-81a6a4212461/scratchpad/pr40-recheck.md"
}

354	+	// blocked by the SAME shared bucket — proving this isn't a
355	+	// per-overflow-identity allowance in disguise, and that the "never
356	+	// zero protection" property actually holds: enough overflow traffic
357	+	// (from any mix of identities) still trips real limiting.
358	+	blockedAfter := -1
359	+	for i := 0; i < 10; i++ {
360	+		ok, err := l.allow(ctx, "flooding-caller", "overflow-identity-2")
361	+		if err != nil {
362	+			t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
363	+		}
364	+		if !ok {
365	+			blockedAfter = i
366	+			break
367	+		}
368	+		l.recordFailure(ctx, "flooding-caller", "overflow-identity-2")
369	+	}
370	+	if blockedAfter == -1 {
371	+		t.Fatalf("the shared overflow bucket never blocked anything across 10 consecutive overflow failures — overflow traffic is getting no protection at all")
372	+	}
373	+}
374	+
375	+// TestIdentityRateLimiter_TrackedIdentityUnaffectedByOverflowPressure
376	+// confirms an identity that DID get its own tracked slot (within the
377	+// cap) is completely unaffected by however much unrelated traffic is
378	+// hammering the shared overflow bucket — the two are genuinely
379	+// independent limiters, not one shared fate.
380	+func TestIdentityRateLimiter_TrackedIdentityUnaffectedByOverflowPressure(t *testing.T) {
381	+	l := NewIdentityRateLimiter()
382	+	ctx := context.Background()
383	+
384	+	// A legitimately-tracked identity, within the cap.
385	+	if allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, "caller", "legit-user"); err != nil || !allowed {
386	+		t.Fatalf("legit-user first attempt: allowed=%v err=%v", allowed, err)
387	+	}
388	+
389	+	// Drive the shared overflow bucket into its blocked state with a
390	+	// flood of DIFFERENT, never-tracked identities under the same cap
391	+	// pressure (reuse the same flood as the sibling test, cheaply, by
392	+	// filling the tracked set first).
393	+	for i := 0; i < maxTrackedIdentities; i++ {
394	+		if _, err := l.allow(ctx, "caller", string(rune(i))+"-fill"); err != nil {
395	+			t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
396	+		}
397	+	}
398	+	for i := 0; i < 10; i++ {
399	+		ok, err := l.allow(ctx, "caller", "overflow-attacker")
400	+		if err != nil {
401	+			t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
402	+		}
403	+		if !ok {
404	+			break
405	+		}
406	+		l.recordFailure(ctx, "caller", "overflow-attacker")
407	+	}
408	+
409	+	// legit-user's OWN tracked bucket must be untouched by any of that.
410	+	allowed, err := l.allow(ctx, "caller", "legit-user")
411	+	if err != nil {
412	+		t.Fatalf("allow: %v", err)
413	+	}
414	+	if !allowed {
415	+		t.Errorf("legit-user (a tracked, within-cap identity) was blocked by unrelated overflow-bucket pressure — the two must be independent")
416	 	}
417	 }

{
  "command": "cat > /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-warrenthompson-Source-LoginID-02-ai-security-architecture/24287bc9-e284-45c4-9fca-81a6a4212461/scratchpad/pr40-recheck2.md << 'EOF'\nIndependent review: Tomasz Wrede (02 Adversary) — PR #40 final re-check at `38a2bdd`\n\n**Does the pre-fill-then-attack scenario now get meaningfully throttled? Yes, in isolation — confirmed by mechanics, not just by the tests' names.**\nPast `maxTrackedIdentities`, `trackOrCheck` now routes an untracked key to `overflow.Allow`/`RecordFailure`/`RecordSuccess` instead of the old unconditional `true, nil`. `TestIdentityRateLimiter_BoundedCardinality_OverflowsToSharedBucket` proves the shared bucket genuinely accumulates and trips across *different* overflowing identities, not per-identity in disguise, and `TestIdentityRateLimiter_TrackedIdentityUnaffectedByOverflowPressure` proves it doesn't leak backward onto already-tracked identities. Nolan's fix to route `recordFailure`/`recordSuccess` through the same `trackOrCheck` choke point (not just `allow`) closes a real adjacent bug — the old code would have let overflow failures grow the wrapped `GrantLimiter`'s own maps unbounded even after `allow()` correctly degraded. So: an attacker who pre-fills the table and then attacks one specific stolen identity, **with no other overflow traffic happening concurrently**, does get genuinely rate-limited by the shared bucket — confirmed, not merely nominal.\n\n**But you're right to ask about the shared bucket itself, and there's a real structural point here, not just a hypothetical.**\n`api.InProcessGrantLimiter.RecordSuccess` clears a key's backoff state entirely — that's its documented, correct behavior for the caller-credentials use case it was originally built for. The overflow bucket uses ONE fixed key (`identityOverflowKey`) shared by every identity that overflows, from every caller, for any reason. That means: **any single success by any legitimately new identity overflowing at the same time resets the entire shared bucket** — including an attacker's own accumulating failures against an unrelated stolen identity that also happens to be in overflow. This is precisely the \"one success anywhere resets everything\" flaw that per-identity buckets were built to eliminate in the first place — it has been reintroduced one tier up, at the overflow population instead of the caller population. None of the new tests exercise this: both overflow tests only ever call `recordFailure`, never `recordSuccess`, so the reset-by-unrelated-success property isn't verified either way.\n\n**Is that acceptable coarsening, per your framing? Conditionally, and the condition isn't established anywhere.**\nIt's acceptable if the overflow population stays genuinely rare — a true edge case reached only under sustained flooding, where an attacker attacking in isolation (no organic overflow traffic) still gets caught, as demonstrated. It stops being acceptable if `maxTrackedIdentities` (10,000, still explicitly flagged in the code as \"this component's own engineering default... not a ruled number\") is small relative to real expected traffic: if this connector's real-world identity-lookup volume routinely exceeds 10,000 distinct identities within `identityTrackedKeyTTL` (1 hour), overflow becomes the *normal* case for new users, not the degraded edge case, and the shared-bucket reset-by-unrelated-success dilution becomes routine rather than adversarial — a real attacker could then hide targeted failures among ordinary background overflow successes essentially permanently, since normal traffic would keep clearing the bucket. Nothing in this PR establishes what \"normal\" traffic volume is expected to be, so there's no basis yet to say which side of that line this system sits on.\n\n**My recommendation, not blocking merge:** this is a legitimate accepted-residual candidate, same shape as the timing floor — should get the same explicit named treatment (\"shared overflow bucket resets on any overflow success; acceptable because overflow is expected to be rare given traffic volume X; revisit if actual volume approaches the cap\") rather than being left as an implicit property nobody has stated out loud. I don't think this blocks the gate — the isolated-attacker scenario I originally raised is genuinely fixed, and \"coarser but never zero\" under flood is a defensible engineering trade-off — but I'd want that reasoning written down before this is treated as fully closed, the same way `connector-security.md` §4 wrote down the timing floor's own accepted-residual reasoning explicitly rather than leaving it implicit.\n\nNet: original scenario — closed, verified by mechanics and test. New structural note — a real, nameable residual (shared-bucket reset dilution at scale), not a blocker, but it should get a ruling and a line in the doc rather than stay silent.\nEOF\ngh pr comment 40 --repo wtvamp/loginid-takehome --body-file /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-warrenthompson-Source-LoginID-02-ai-security-architecture/24287bc9-e284-45c4-9fca-81a6a4212461/scratchpad/pr40-recheck2.md"

Posted: https://github.com/wtvamp/loginid-takehome/pull/40#issuecomment-5653735240 Same text for PM/Jira relay: --- **Independent review: Tomasz Wrede (02 Adversary) — PR #40 final re-check at `38a2bdd`** **Does the pre-fill-then-attack scenario get meaningfully throttled? Yes, in isolation — confirmed by mechanics.** Past `maxTrackedIdentities`, new keys route to `overflow.Allow`/`RecordFailure`/`RecordSuccess` instead of unconditional bypass. Tests confirm the shared bucket genuinely accumulates across different overflowing identities and doesn't leak onto already-tracked ones. Nolan's fix routing `recordFailure`/`recordSuccess` through the same tracking choke point closes an adjacent real bug (unbounded map growth on failures alone). An attacker pre-filling then attacking one stolen identity, with no concurrent overflow traffic, does get genuinely rate-limited now. **But there's a real structural point on the shared bucket itself.** `GrantLimiter.RecordSuccess` clears a key's state entirely — correct for its original use case. The overflow bucket is one fixed key shared by every overflowing identity from every caller, so any single legitimate success anywhere in overflow resets the whole bucket, including an attacker's unrelated accumulating failures also sitting in overflow. That's the exact "one success resets everything" flaw per-identity buckets were built to eliminate — reintroduced one tier up. No test exercises this (`recordSuccess` is never called in either new overflow test). **Is that acceptable coarsening? Conditionally, and the condition isn't established.** Fine if overflow stays a rare edge case. Not fine if real traffic routinely exceeds 10,000 distinct identities/hour, at which point overflow becomes the normal case and the reset-by-unrelated-success dilution becomes routine rather than adversarial. Nothing in this PR states expected traffic volume, so there's no basis to know which side of that line this sits on. **Recommendation, not blocking:** this is a legitimate accepted-residual candidate, same shape as the timing floor — deserves the same explicit named ruling ("acceptable because volume X; revisit if Y") rather than staying implicit. I don't think it blocks the gate — the scenario I originally raised is genuinely fixed, and "coarser but never zero" under flood is a defensible trade-off — but it should get written down, not left silent. Net: original scenario closed and verified. New structural note flagged, not a blocker.
